## management-governance — module synthesis

_Source: `99_management-governance-synthesis.md`_

# Management-Governance Module — NU (Synthesis)

## Abstract

Nu appears operationally competent but only partly aligned with outside shareholders. Capital allocation is mixed: diluted EPS rose 175.6% from FY23 to FY25 and a US$500.4 million buyback reduced end-period issued shares 0.5%, yet returns on acquisitions and reinvestment are not disclosed. Broad employee equity and a three-year clawback help, but individual executive scorecards are opaque and insiders were net open-market sellers. The central risk is founder David Vélez’s 74.4% voting control through 20-for-1 Class B shares while serving as CEO and chair. Clean audited opinions and no hard disqualifier keep the call from being negative, but limited legal and network sweeps leave the verdict Standard / mixed.

## 1. Stewardship Verdict

- **Verdict:** Standard / mixed
- **NON-NEGOTIABLE GATE: PASS** — no hard disqualifier, no Critical red flag, no Disqualifying-graded controller/CEO/CFO/chair, and no material undisclosed legal matter. The checklist answered 114 of 179 registry items.
- **Hard disqualifier flagged (business-model/01)?** N — the scan found no trigger across its eight tests. [Business-Model Disqualifier Scan — NU, §§1–3, 2026-08-30]
- Management quality /100: **68**
- Capital allocation /100: **65**
- Incentive alignment /100: **52**
- Shareholder friendliness /100: **46**
- Disclosure candor /100: **68**
- People & network integrity /100: **55** (raw 58; capped from the query-budget shortfall)
- Audit & assurance quality /100: **53**
- RPT & leakage risk /100, **higher = worse:** **36**
- Contingent-liability risk /100, **higher = worse:** **27**
- Accounting-forensics risk /100, **higher = worse:** **29**
- Legal & regulatory risk /100, **higher = worse:** **40**
- Governance risk /100, **higher = worse:** **55** — synthesis judgment anchored to founder entrenchment and two unique formal red flags, offset by no proven control abuse, leakage, hard disqualifier, or gate failure.
- Data quality /100: **74**
- Overall usefulness /100: **72**
- **Checklist coverage:** **114/179 (63.7%)** — 31 Green / 77 Amber / 6 Red / 65 N-A (47 Insufficient Data and 18 Not Applicable)
- Insider ownership: CIQ reports 22.80% insider ownership; the filing attributes 18.6% economic ownership and 74.4% voting power to Vélez. Open-market activity was 0 buys and 4 sales, net −592,707 shares. [FY25 Form 20-F, Item 7A, pp.202–203; CIQ Ownership and Insider Trading exports, through 2026-08-26 — vendor]
- Biggest governance signal: one founder is CEO and chair, controls 74.4% of votes through 20:1 Class B shares, and holds nomination and consent rights; this is entrenchment, not evidence of misconduct. [FY25 Form 20-F, Items 6A, 7A and 10B]
- **Checklist Risk:** max(36 RPT, 27 contingent-liability, 29 accounting-forensics, 40 legal/regulatory) = **40**, bound by legal/regulatory coverage.
- **Governance Score /100:** 0.14×65 + 0.11×52 + 0.11×46 + 0.10×68 + 0.11×68 + 0.09×53 + 0.11×55 + 0.11×(100−55) + 0.12×(100−40) = **57.13**, reported as **57**.
- **Confidence-Adjusted Governance Score /100:** 57.13 × 68% = **38.85**, reported as **39**.
- **Governance Rating:** **Watchlist**
- **Confidence Score /100:** **68** — primary filings are strong, but legal/person/entity sweeps are incomplete and two upstream output-schema defects required conservative reconciliation.
- **Red-Flag Count / Critical Red-Flag Count:** **2 / 0** unique canonical flags.

## 1A. Module Disconfirmation

- **Strongest bear point:** Vélez’s 18.6% economic stake carries 74.4% of votes, a 55.8-point wedge, while he is both CEO and chair; public Class A holders cannot independently replace the board or block major actions. [FY25 Form 20-F, Items 6A, 7A and 10B]
- **Strongest bull point:** FY23–FY25 consolidated audits were unqualified, FY25 internal control over financial reporting was effective, disclosed non-audit fees were 7.8% of audit fees, and the hard-disqualifier scan found no trigger. [FY25 Form 20-F, auditor report and Item 16C; Business-Model Disqualifier Scan — NU, §1]
- **Single killer risk:** the controller could use nomination, consent, dilution or transaction powers against Class A holders; no abuse is proven today, but minority blocking power is weak. [FY25 Form 20-F, Items 6A and 10B]
- **Disconfirming evidence already visible:** fully independent Audit/Risk membership, a lead independent director, group-owned core trademarks, tiny known director-linked balances and no proven leakage argue against treating structural control as present misconduct. [FY25 Form 20-F, Items 6A and Note 28; INPI process 925913570, 2024-10-15]

| A3-04 | Differential voting rights | 20 Class B votes per share versus 1 for Class A | High | None assigned upstream; structural checklist Red, non-gate | Monitor any amendment, sunset or independent minority-protection measure. |
| A4-02 | Audit firm calibre | 3 PCAOB disciplinary releases listed on firm profile | High | RF-AUD-007 | Ask KPMG/Audit Committee whether the 2025 remedial actions are complete and whether any finding touched the NU engagement. |
| A4-13 | Regulator discipline of firm/partner | 700000 US$ PCAOB civil penalty in 2025 | High | RF-AUD-007 | Obtain a written Audit Committee/KPMG statement on remediation and NU-engagement impact. |

The six registry Reds are not six unique canonical red flags. A4-02 and A4-13 are two checklist views of one RF-AUD-007 event. A1-02, A1-03, A3-03 and A3-04 are structural Red tests for combined roles and voting asymmetry; the owner supplied no canonical RF ID, so this synthesis does not invent one.

## 3. Reconciliation

- **Economic ownership versus minority alignment:** 04 records meaningful insider economics, but the 55.8-point control/economic wedge and 20:1 votes make the more conservative 05 minority-rights read decisive. Shareholder friendliness remains 46, not a positive score derived from ownership alone. [FY25 Form 20-F, Item 7A]
- **Independent board labels versus control:** 7 of 9 official directors are designated independent and Audit/Risk is fully independent, but the controller determines ordinary votes and has special nomination/consent rights. Formal independence does not cancel entrenchment. [FY25 Form 20-F, Items 6A and 10B]
- **Candor versus adjusted framing:** management’s Q1 timing bridge was direct and later matched Q2, but recurring stock-compensation exclusion lifts FY25 adjusted profit by US$203.7m, or 7.1%. This supports a mixed 68 rather than a high-trust read. [Q1/Q2 2026 calls; FY25 Form 20-F, Item 5A]
- **Clean issuer audits versus audit-firm discipline:** NU’s opinions and FY25 ICFR are clean; the PCAOB order concerns other issuer audits. RF-AUD-007 therefore lowers assurance quality without asserting a defect in NU’s audit. [FY25 Form 20-F, auditor report; PCAOB Release 105-2025-008, 2025-03-11]
- **Small quantified contingencies versus large unallocated claim:** the US$54.018m legal-exposure build is 0.41% of net worth, but the COP9.3tn GMF claim is an all-defendant amount across 52 entities and cannot be assigned to Nu. It stays an Amber uncertainty, not a fabricated pro-rata liability. [H1 FY26 Interim Report, Note 25; FY25 Form 20-F, Item 8A]
- **Cash-flow optics versus bank accounting:** H1 FY26 CFO was negative, but the non-financial CFO/PAT and Beneish/Dechow tests are invalid for a deposit-funded lender. The bank overlay’s +149bp ECL-intensity rise is the comparable warning; no manipulation flag fires. [H1 FY26 Interim Report; Management-Governance MODULE_RULES, financials overlay]
- **People cap:** 07 reported 58 and cited only max-65/max-70 caps. Its Scope-Boundary register, however, labels several E-A branches budget_exhausted, including founder/KMP-controlled holder vehicles. The exact rule applies max 55, so synthesis uses 55. [People & Network Integrity Dossiers — NU, §§0 and 5; MODULE_RULES, query-budget exhaustion cap]
- **Registry duplication:** A6-03, A6-09, A9-01 and A9-09 had multiple owner rows. The checklist uses the owner’s primary aggregate row and retains every detail row in §2A and governance_findings.csv.

**Forensic tag propagation:** RF-DISC-002 (recurring “one-off” / aggressive non-GAAP add-backs) fired and is carried below. RF-AUD-007 also fired. No RF-MGT-005, RF-DISC-001 or RF-REG-002 fired.

## 5. Stewardship Summary

Management has met the small set of mature operating promises, and diluted earnings per share rose 175.6% from FY23 to FY25, but the evidence cannot attribute that gain to each capital use. The new buyback reduced end-period shares, while the weighted diluted count still rose 0.2% because purchases began late; full-year per-share delivery remains open. Broad employee equity and a clawback point toward ownership, but undisclosed executive scorecards leave it unclear whether pay rewards returns or merely continued service and growth. Minority holders have weak practical protection because the founder controls 74.4% of votes and is CEO and chair, although independent committees, clean issuer audits and no proven leakage are meaningful offsets. The main reason for caution is therefore structural control combined with incomplete external integrity/legal coverage, not a proven fraud or related-party extraction.

## 6. What Would Change The Stewardship Verdict?

| Current Verdict | What Would Strengthen It | What Would Weaken It | Data Needed |
|---|---|---|---|
| Standard / mixed | A sunset or Class-A/minority gate on super-votes; named return-based pay weights and outcomes; full-year buyback reducing diluted shares; complete person/entity sweeps with no material fact; clean KPMG remediation evidence | Controller-favored dilution/RPT/M&A; unexplained gatekeeper exit; wider recurring adjusted-profit gap; a material undisclosed case; Material/Disqualifying controller/KMP fact; audit or ICFR weakness | Verified registry/beneficial-owner and legal-screen package for Rua California and the named Junqueira/Pereira holder vehicles; itemised 2026 AGM votes; FY26 executive scorecards; FY26 share-count and adjusted-profit bridges |

## 7. Note To The Final Synthesizer

- Stewardship is **Standard / mixed**: operating delivery and clean issuer audits are offset by weak minority rights and incomplete external coverage; the strongest evidence is Vélez’s 18.6% economic stake carrying 74.4% of votes while he is CEO and chair.
- **Non-Negotiable Gate: PASS with 114/179 checklist items answered.** No hard disqualifier, Critical flag, Disqualifying gate person, or material undisclosed legal matter is proven.
- Capital allocation has produced higher diluted EPS and an early end-period share reduction, but returns on Hyperplane, associates, Banco Porto Real and software/market expansion are not disclosed.
- Employee equity is broad and a clawback exists, but executive metrics, weights and individual payouts are opaque; economic ownership does not overcome the super-vote wedge.
- People read: 24 Clean on own record, 0 Material, 0 Disqualifying; every grade is coverage-limited. The highest-risk item is unswept holder-vehicle/entity coverage, not an open §24 Filter 1 signal.
- Checklist read: 63.7% answered; Reds are A1-02, A1-03, A3-03, A3-04, A4-02 and A4-13. Legal/regulatory risk at 40 is the binding Checklist Risk.
- Biggest formal flags: RF-AUD-007 High, non-gate; RF-DISC-002 Medium, non-gate. Structural founder control is the biggest governance risk but is not itself a misconduct finding.
- Business-model/01 hard disqualifier: **none; “Any disqualifier triggered: N.”**
- §24 rejector filters: turnaround N; serial acquirer N; structurally unaligned owner RF-OWN-004 N on available evidence; unresolved integrity signal N. No rejector-filter cap applies.
- Partial-data caps: legal/person/database coverage limits confidence to 70 and floors Legal risk at 40; query-budget exhaustion cuts People & network integrity from 58 to 55. Actual confidence is 68.
- Highest-value next data request: a verified corporate-registry and beneficial-owner extract, followed by identifier-based court/regulator/sanctions checks, for Rua California Ltd. and the disclosed Junqueira/Pereira holder vehicles.
- **This module supersedes business-model/11_capital-allocation-governance. Treat this module’s verdict and scores as the primary governance read.**

## 8. Simple Summary

- Management met the small number of mature promises, but there is not enough formal guidance to call the forecasting record proven.
- Diluted EPS rose 175.6% from FY23 to FY25; the new buyback lowered end-period shares, but full-year diluted shares have not yet fallen.
- Pay uses broad equity and a clawback, but investors cannot see individual executive measures, weights or payouts.
- Insiders own 22.80% on the vendor measure; Vélez has 18.6% of the economics and 74.4% of votes. Open-market activity was four sales and no buys.
- Minority protection is weak because the founder is CEO/chair and controls votes, nominations and major-action consents.
- Management was candid about one favorable timing distortion, but adjusted profit still excludes recurring stock compensation.
- No hard disqualifier or Critical red flag was found.
- The module is useful, but its Watchlist rating carries a coverage cap: person, entity and legal sweeps are incomplete.

# Governance Data Triage — NU

**Run date:** 2026-08-30. `data/NU/` contains 111 source files. The existing canonical extraction manifest contains 111 source entries, 84 workbook-tab extracts and 64 non-workbook extracts; every manifest entry is `ok`. A re-run of `extract_pool.py` was attempted but the local macOS command-line-tools prerequisite is unavailable in this non-GUI environment. This does not change the verdict because the already-present canonical manifest and every required extract are available and mechanically readable. No manifest entry is `fail`, `fallback-text`, `missing-dependency`, or `gdrive-pointer`.

## 6. Sufficiency Verdict

- **Verdict:** Sufficient.
- **Reason:** The pool contains the foreign-private-issuer equivalents of proxy/compensation, ownership and insider information, board and related-party disclosure, audited annual filings for five consecutive years, a current audited interim review, and a five-year transcript record. The lack of a US domestic DEF 14A is not a gap because Nu is a SEC foreign private issuer and the relevant disclosures appear in its Form 20-F.
- **Specialists that can run:** management track record; capital allocation; incentives; ownership; board; candor; people dossiers; audit quality; RPT/group forensics; contingent liabilities; accounting forensics; regulatory/legal.
- **Hard disqualifier already flagged by business-model/01_disqualifier-scan?** N — the completed scan records no trigger across its eight tests and no verdict lock. It also records missing pledged-share and RPT-flow numerators as “not assessable,” not as zero. [business-model/01_disqualifier-scan, §§1–3]
- **Active partial-data caps:** None at the module level. Agent 05 should mark AGM voting-result analysis Not Available because itemised results are absent. Agent 03 should label individual incentive weights as not fully disclosed where the 20-F only describes the framework. Agents 07 and 12 must apply their coverage caps only if their required external registry/court/regulator sweeps actually fail or are unreachable.
- **Critical missing items:** Itemised 2026 AGM voting results and a discrete remuneration report with individual metric weights/targets; neither prevents the dedicated module from running.
- **Single highest-value missing document:** 2026 AGM voting results / formal voting tabulation.

**Key current evidence for later agents:** David Vélez beneficially owned 88.3% of Class B shares and 74.4% of total voting power at the 20-F ownership cut-off; Class B carries 20 votes per share and the shareholder agreement provides director-nomination and consent rights at stated voting thresholds [FY25 Form 20-F, Item 7A pp.202–203; Item 6A pp.192–193]. This is a control-structure fact, not a governance verdict. CFO Rob Livingston succeeded Guilherme Lago on 2026-07-13; Lago remains a special adviser [Q2 2026 earnings call, p.6; CIQ Corporate Timeline, 2026-06-01].

**Vendor sidecar reconciliation:** `ciq_facts.json` reports `insider_net_activity` of +4,015,012 shares across 14 acquisitions and 11 dispositions through 2026-08-26, while open-market-only activity is −592,707 shares across four sales; the separate CIQ trade export supports the event basis. These are tier-5 vendor reads, not filing facts. Later agents must distinguish RSU/other acquisitions from open-market trading and cite the vendor export when using them.

# Management & Track Record — NU

## 1. Key Executives

| Role | Name | Appointed | Tenure | Relevant Background | Source |
|---|---|---:|---:|---|---|
| CEO / chair / founder | David Vélez Osorno | Founded Nu in 2013 | ~13 years | Former Sequoia Capital partner for Latin America; earlier Goldman Sachs, Morgan Stanley and General Atlantic roles. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp. 187–188] |
| CFO | Rob Livingston | 2026-07-13 | ~0.1 years | Former Visa North America CFO; previously held Visa finance / IR and regional CFO roles and spent 18 years at Capital One. | [Capital IQ Professionals, Rob Livingston profile, data as of 2026-08-29; Capital IQ Corporate Timeline, 2026-06-01] |
| Co-founder / Chief Growth Officer / U.S. CEO | Cristina Helena Zingaretti Junqueira | Co-founded Nu in 2013; CGO since 2022-08 | ~13 years at Nu | Former Itaú Unibanco consumer-credit, cards and marketing executive. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp. 190–191; Capital IQ Professionals, Cristina Junqueira profile, data as of 2026-08-29] |
| Chief Risk Officer | Henrique Camossa Saldanha Fragelli | 2018 | ~8 years | Former HSBC traded-portfolio analytics leader; owns credit, market, liquidity, model, operational, IT, conduct and AML risk functions. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp. 190–192] |
| LATAM CEO | Livia Martines Chanes | Joined Nu in 2020; LATAM CEO from 2026-07 | ~6 years at Nu | Former Itaú technology-business executive and McKinsey partner; was Brazil CEO from 2024-01 to 2026-07. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, p. 192; Capital IQ Professionals, Livia Chanes profile, data as of 2026-08-29] |

Vélez is a founder-controller and CEO-chair, so he is an owner-operator in the control sense. As of 2026-03-01, he beneficially owned 88.3% of Class B shares, equal to 74.4% of total voting power; this report does not infer his economic ownership percentage from voting rights. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, p. 203]

## 2. Management Stability

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Senior departures / role changes (last 3 years) | The FY2024 management table included President/COO Youssef Lahrech and CTO Vitor Guarino Olivier; neither appeared in the FY2025 table. The pool does not identify the dates, reasons, or succession disclosure for those changes. That is an information gap, not evidence of misconduct. | [FY2024 Form 20-F, Item 6A, p. 272; FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp. 190–192] |
| CFO turnover | One planned handover after Guilherme Lago's five years as CFO and seven years at Nu: Rob Livingston became CFO on 2026-07-13; Lago supported the handover through 2026-08-31 and remains special adviser. This is not serial CFO churn, but Livingston's tenure is too short to judge. | [Q2 2026 earnings-call transcript, pp. 6–8; Capital IQ Corporate Timeline, 2026-06-01] |
| CEO succession / continuity | Vélez has held CEO since founding. The annual management tables also show continuity in the CFO role through FY2025 and the CRO role since 2018; the FY2026 CFO change is the first identified finance handover in the available annual-record comparison. | [FY2023 Form 20-F, Item 6A, management team; FY2024 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp. 272–273; FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp. 190–192; Q2 2026 earnings-call transcript, pp. 6–8] |

The core five-person operating group used for the team-vintage test (CEO, CFO, CGO, CRO and LATAM CEO) has a median company tenure of about eight years: 13, 0.1, 13, 8 and 6 years respectively. The new CFO is an orderly addition to an otherwise long-tenured core, rather than evidence of serial turnover. The broader officer roster has been reshaped, however, and the two unclassified FY2024-to-FY2025 role changes stop this from being a fully clean stability read. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp. 190–192; Capital IQ Professionals, data as of 2026-08-29]

## 3. Stated Strategy & Targets

Management describes an earnings model built on more active customers, higher ARPAC (average monthly revenue per active customer), and a scalable operating platform. It is investing in Brazil, Mexico and Colombia while treating U.S. entry as a bounded option rather than committing to unrestricted expansion. [Q2 2026 earnings presentation, slides 6 and 28; Q1 2026 earnings-call transcript, pp. 8–9]

The checkable operating targets are cost to serve at or below US$1 per active customer, FY2026 efficiency ratio of about 20%, and a U.S. investment drag below 100 basis points of the consolidated efficiency ratio in each of 2026 and 2027. Management gives no formal revenue, IFRS EPS, EBIT or capex target; that limits forecast-accuracy testing. [Q4 2023 earnings-call transcript, p. 8; Q1 2026 earnings-call transcript, pp. 8–9; Q2 2026 earnings-call transcript, pp. 8–11]

## 4. Promise vs Delivery

| Prior Promise / Target | Period Set | Actual Outcome | Kept / Missed | Source |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Keep cost to serve at or below US$1 per active customer for the foreseeable future. | Q4 2023 | US$0.80 in Q4 2024 and US$1.00 in Q2 2026. The latter equals, rather than improves on, the ceiling. | Kept | [Q4 2023 earnings-call transcript, p. 8; Q4 2024 earnings-call transcript, p. 8; Q2 2026 earnings presentation, slide 28] |
| Reverse the Q4 2023 efficiency-ratio increase caused by marketing phasing and cloud costs; management said the roughly 170bp effect would reverse in coming quarters. | Q4 2023 | Efficiency ratio fell from 36.0% in Q4 2023 to 32.1% in Q1 2024, a 390bp reduction. | Kept, on the stated short-term measure | [Q4 2023 earnings-call transcript, p. 18; Q1 2024 earnings-call transcript, p. 8] |
| FY2026 efficiency ratio to average about 20%; Q1's 17.6% was explicitly not a run rate. | Q1 2026 | Q2 2026 was 19.5%, consistent with normalization, but the full-year outcome is not yet available. | Not yet assessable | [Q1 2026 earnings-call transcript, p. 8; Q2 2026 earnings-call transcript, p. 8] |
| U.S. entry to add less than 100bp to consolidated efficiency ratio in each of 2026 and 2027, with further investment contingent on product-market fit. | Q1 2026 | No full-year 2026 result is available. | Not yet assessable | [Q1 2026 earnings-call transcript, p. 9] |
| IFRS effective tax rate to converge toward 15%–20% for the remainder of 2026. | Q1 2026 | Q2 tax expense of US$175.224m divided by US$1,236.313m pre-tax income equals 14.2%. One quarter is below the stated range and cannot settle a remainder-of-year target. | Not yet assessable | [Q1 2026 earnings-call transcript, p. 8; Q2 2026 Unaudited Interim Condensed Consolidated Financial Statements, Note 30, p. 36] |

The two mature operating commitments located in the transcript archive were met. That is a narrow sample and should not be presented as a proven record of formal revenue or EPS guidance, because Nu does not give such targets. The Q1 2026 warning that 17.6% efficiency was temporary was borne out by Q2's 19.5%, which supports candor on that one metric but not a broad assessment of disclosure quality. [Q1 2026 earnings-call transcript, p. 8; Q2 2026 earnings-call transcript, p. 8]

## 4A. Turnaround & Integrity Tests (CLAUDE.md §24, Filters 1 & 2)

| Test | Finding | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Is the thesis a turnaround (struggling business + new management / new plan)? | No. This is a founder-led expansion business, not a new-management rescue. Q2 2026 net income was US$1.061bn, up 49% year on year on an FX-neutral basis, while the founder CEO remained in place. | [Q2 2026 earnings-call transcript, pp. 6 and 8; FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp. 187–188] |
| If yes: years of *proven* operating inflection actually delivered (not promised) | Not applicable — no turnaround claim is being credited. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp. 187–192] |
| Résumé / plan / consultant deck cited as the reason to believe? (not evidence) | No. The positive read rests only on the measured cost and efficiency outcomes above; the new CFO's résumé is not treated as evidence of execution. | [Capital IQ Professionals, Rob Livingston profile, data as of 2026-08-29; Q4 2023 and Q1 2024 earnings-call transcripts, pp. 18 and 8] |
| Routed integrity "buzz" from `business-model/01_disqualifier-scan` (unverified adverse signal) | No unverified adverse integrity signal was routed by the available scan. No `RF-MGT-005` is emitted. | [Business-model Disqualifier Scan — NU, §§1–3, 2026-08-30] |

## 5. Management Read

Vélez's 13-year founder tenure and an eight-year median company tenure across the five-person operating core support a stable control center; the CFO replacement was planned and supported by the former CFO. The evidence supports promises mostly kept only for two concrete operating commitments, not a broad claim about earnings guidance, because management supplies no formal revenue, EPS, EBIT or capex targets. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp. 187–192; Q2 2026 earnings-call transcript, pp. 6–8]

The biggest management signal is the combination of an orderly CFO handover with unclassified previous role changes: the FY2024 President/COO and CTO no longer appeared in the FY2025 officer table, and the available pool does not explain those changes. This is an Amber disclosure and succession-monitoring item, not a finding of improper conduct. [FY2024 Form 20-F, Item 6A, p. 272; FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp. 190–192]

## Management Quality Score

| Component | Score | Max Score | Evidence |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Stability and tenure | 15 | 20 | 13-year founder CEO, 8-year median core-team tenure, and one planned CFO handover; offset by two unclassified prior roster changes. [01-001 to 01-005] |
| Promise-vs-delivery | 20 | 30 | Cost-to-serve ceiling and Q4 2023 efficiency-normalisation commitment were met; FY2026 targets remain open and no formal revenue/EPS guide exists. [01-006 to 01-009] |
| Execution record | 18 | 25 | The mature operating commitments were delivered; Q2 2026 efficiency was 19.5%, but the evidence does not support a broader management forecast-accuracy claim. [01-006 to 01-008] |
| Succession / depth | 10 | 15 | Named functional leadership supports depth, but no formal successor slate and incomplete disclosure around prior role changes limit the score. [01-002, 01-004, 01-005] |
| Transparency around misses | 5 | 10 | Management explicitly said Q1 2026 efficiency was not a run rate and Q2 normalised; absence of formal revenue/EPS guidance limits the broader candor test. [01-008, 01-009] |
| Total | 68 | 100 | Solid operating evidence but limited guidance accountability and incomplete senior-transition detail. |

No hard partial-data cap applies: the pool contains multi-year annual filings and a dated transcript archive with checkable operating statements. The 68 score nevertheless embeds the limited promise sample; it must not be read as a comprehensive forecast-accuracy score.

## management-governance / 02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md

_Source: `02_capital-allocation-scorecard.md`_

# Capital-Allocation Scorecard — NU

## 1. Uses of Capital (3–5 years)

Nu Holdings reports under IFRS Accounting Standards in U.S. dollars and has a 31 December year-end. Amounts below are US$ millions, except shares and per-share data. The window is FY2023 through H1 FY2026, a 3.5-year period. Cumulative cash from operating activities (CFO) is US$5,923.649m: FY2023 US$1,266.189m + FY2024 US$2,399.044m + FY2025 US$3,500.464m − H1 FY2026 US$1,242.048m. The H1 outflow is driven by a deposit-taking lender's balance-sheet movements; CFO and the mechanically calculated FCF of US$5,130.413m (CFO less cash capex) are not distributable cash measures for a bank. The percentages are therefore cash-use context, not payout capacity. [FY2025 Form 20-F, FY ended 2025-12-31, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, pp.F14–F15; H1 2026 Unaudited Interim Condensed Consolidated Financial Statements, six months ended 2026-06-30, Statement of Cash Flows, p.12]

| Use | Cumulative Amount | Currency | % of CFO (or FCF) | Source |
|---|---:|---|---:|---|
| Acquisitions (net) | 7.176 cash only; equity consideration not dollar-disclosed | USD m | 0.1% of CFO | FY2024/H1 cash-flow lines show US$5.637m/FY2024 and US$1.539m/FY2025 cash paid for subsidiaries net of acquired cash; share-issuance rows are non-cash and are not added to this cash total. [FY2025 Form 20-F, FY2025, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.F15; Note 31, pp.F84–F86] |
| Investments in associates | 99.365 | USD m | 1.7% of CFO | [FY2025 Form 20-F, FY2025, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.F15] |
| Buybacks | 500.393 | USD m | 8.4% of CFO | H1 FY2026 only. [H1 2026 Unaudited Interim Condensed Consolidated Financial Statements, Note 31(e), p.39] |
| Dividends | 0 | USD m | 0.0% of CFO | Nu has never declared or paid a cash dividend. [FY2025 Form 20-F, FY2025, Item 8.A “Dividend and Dividend Policy,” p.206] |
| Capex | 793.236 | USD m | 13.4% of CFO | Cash PP&E plus intangible-development spend: US$177.003m/FY2023 + US$174.990m/FY2024 + US$340.769m/FY2025 + US$100.474m/H1 FY2026. [FY2025 Form 20-F, FY2025, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.F15; H1 2026 Unaudited Interim Condensed Consolidated Financial Statements, Statement of Cash Flows, p.12] |
| Debt repayment | 1,608.704 gross repayments | USD m | 27.2% of CFO | Gross repayments, not net debt reduction: proceeds were US$5,127.559m over the same window, leaving a US$3,518.855m net financing inflow. [FY2025 Form 20-F, FY2025, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.F15; H1 2026 Unaudited Interim Condensed Consolidated Financial Statements, Statement of Cash Flows, p.12] |

The acquisition, associate-investment, capex and debt figures are separate cash-flow categories. They are not summed into a single “investment” total because they are different uses and, for M&A, the filings do not give a complete dollar value for stock consideration. The primary-basis balance-sheet read is cash of US$13.552bn less US$4.682bn borrowings and financing, US$1.058bn repurchase agreements and US$0.066bn lease liabilities = US$7.745bn strict net cash at 30 June 2026. Capital IQ instead reports US$5.897bn total debt and US$(9.274)bn net debt on its vendor basis; those figures are not substituted for the filing-defined calculation. [H1 2026 Unaudited Interim Condensed Consolidated Financial Statements, Statements of Financial Position, p.8; Notes 11, 21 and 24, pp.14, 27 and 32; CIQ Financials→Balance Sheet “Total Debt” and “Net Debt” [Jun-30-2026] — vendor basis, deterministic `ciq_facts.json` read]

## 2. M&A Scorecard

| Deal | Year | Price | Disclosed Rationale / Synergy | Delivered? | Opportunity Cost (divestitures forced / focus lost / options foregone) | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---|---|---|---|
| Hyperplane | 2024 | Not disclosed | U.S. AI platform; management says it should accelerate AI development across products. | Not proven from available data: the filing gives no stand-alone revenue, earnings, return-on-investment or synergy result. | No forced divestiture is identified in the reviewed filing sections. Management attention and the alternative use of capital cannot be quantified. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, FY2025, Business Overview, pp.27–28; Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.F15] |
| Completed subsidiary acquisitions and associate investment | FY2023–FY2025 | US$7.176m cash acquisition outflow net of acquired cash, plus US$99.365m associates investment; stock consideration value not disclosed | The source does not connect the cash-flow aggregate to a per-deal return target. | Not proven from available data. Neither deal-level returns nor realised synergies are disclosed. | No forced divestiture is identified in the reviewed filing sections; this is not evidence that there was no opportunity cost. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, FY2025, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.F15; Note 31, pp.F84–F86] |
| Banco Porto Real de Investimentos | 2026 announced; pending approval | Not disclosed | Add a Brazilian banking licence to Nu Pagamentos’ financial conglomerate. | Not applicable yet: completion remains subject to Brazilian Central Bank approval. | Price, funding source, integration costs and opportunity cost are not disclosed. | [H1 2026 Unaudited Interim Condensed Consolidated Financial Statements, Note 35(b), p.43] |

**Serial-acquirer + opportunity-cost test (CLAUDE.md §24, Filter 4).** The available record shows one completed capability acquisition (Hyperplane), small reported cash-acquisition flows, one associate investment and a pending licence acquisition; it does not establish multiple material, debt-funded acquisitions near Nu’s own value. Therefore RF-CAP-004 and its 50-point M&A cap do not apply. That is not a positive return finding: the consideration and delivery evidence are too thin to give M&A more than a middle score. No filing evidence reviewed shows a deal-driven divestiture, but management-focus and foregone organic-investment costs are not measured. [FY2025 Form 20-F, FY2025, Business Overview, pp.27–28; Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.F15; H1 2026 Unaudited Interim Condensed Consolidated Financial Statements, Note 35(b), p.43]

## 4. Dividends & Organic Reinvestment

| Metric | Value | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Payout ratio | 0.0%: Nu has never paid a cash dividend. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, FY2025, Item 8.A “Dividend and Dividend Policy,” p.206] |
| Dividend coverage (FCF) | Not applicable: no dividend was paid. FY2023–H1 FY2026 mechanical CFO less cash capex was US$5.130bn, but it is not distributable FCF for this deposit-taking lender. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, FY2025, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, pp.F14–F15; H1 2026 Unaudited Interim Condensed Consolidated Financial Statements, Statement of Cash Flows, p.12] |
| Documented dividend policy exists? Followed for ≥4 years? (A10-01) | No. The company explicitly has not adopted a dividend policy and says it expects to retain earnings; this is not an erratic cut or a debt-funded payout. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, FY2025, Item 8.A “Dividend and Dividend Policy,” p.206] |
| Incremental ROIC (Δ NOPAT / Δ invested capital) | Not assessable. NOPAT and an operating “invested capital” base are not reported on a comparable basis, and this deposit-funded lender’s balance sheet makes the standard industrial-company calculation misleading. It is not replaced with a guessed number. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, FY2025, Consolidated Statements of Financial Position and Cash Flows, pp.F8–F15] |

Organic spending is visible—US$793.236m of cash capex in the 3.5-year window—and diluted EPS rose from US$0.2121 in FY2023 to US$0.5846 in FY2025 (+175.6%). That is a favourable per-share outcome, but the filings do not demonstrate that capex, rather than credit growth, pricing, risk, funding or other operating drivers, caused it. [FY2025 Form 20-F, FY2025, Consolidated Statements of Cash Flows, p.F15; Consolidated Statements of Income, pp.F6–F7; Note 9, pp.F45–F46]

| Test | Finding | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Mechanism rationale stated (dividend vs buyback, who benefits)? | The board approved a US$1.0bn, 12-month Class A programme. It says timing and amount depend on market conditions, price, liquidity and other capital-allocation opportunities. The filing does not provide an explicit dividend-versus-buyback comparison or a shareholder-group benefit analysis. | [H1 2026 Unaudited Interim Condensed Consolidated Financial Statements, Note 31(e), p.39] |
| Promoter participation in any buyback (tender/open-market) | The programme permits open-market or privately negotiated Class A purchases, not a tender. The H1 filing does not identify sellers, so promoter/insider participation is not proven from available data. | [H1 2026 Unaudited Interim Condensed Consolidated Financial Statements, Note 31(e), p.39] |
| Buyback timing vs promoter pledge / stake changes | Not assessable. The available disqualifier scan found no pledged-share numerator; an unreported numerator is not a reported zero. | [Business-model Disqualifier Scan — NU, 2026-08-30, §1] |

## 5. Capital-Allocation Read

**Mixed.** Organic spending coincided with diluted EPS rising 175.6% from FY2023 to FY2025, but an incremental-ROIC calculation is not supportable for this lender and the filing does not isolate the contribution of the US$793.236m capex programme. The best observed capital-return action is the H1 FY2026 US$500.393m Class A buyback: it reduced end-period shares by 24.985m, or 0.5%, after equity issuance, although the weighted diluted share count was still 0.2% higher year on year because execution started late. The weakest area is M&A evidence: Hyperplane’s price and delivery, the associate investment’s outcome, and Banco Porto Real’s consideration are not disclosed, so value creation from these uses is not proven. The single biggest signal is a real but early buyback funded from a balance sheet with US$7.745bn strict net cash; it deserves monitoring for duration, seller participation and value discipline rather than credit for an unproven long-term record.

| Balance-sheet prudence | 9 | 10 | US$7.745bn strict net cash on the stated scope; funding-basis reconciliation still matters for a deposit-taking lender. [02-010] |
| Per-share value creation | 6 | 10 | FY2023–FY2025 diluted EPS rose 175.6%, while share-count benefits from the new buyback are not yet established over a full year. [02-007, 02-011] |
| **Total** | **65** | **100** | **Mixed: good organic/per-share result and early buyback, capped by unmeasured returns on M&A and reinvestment.** |

No multi-year-history cap applies: audited FY2023–FY2025 and H1 FY2026 records are available. The score does not assume acquisition returns, intrinsic-value discipline or a lender-style incremental ROIC where the filing cannot support them.

## management-governance / 03_incentives-and-compensation.md

_Source: `03_incentives-and-compensation.md`_

# Incentives & Compensation — NU

**Reporting currency and period:** USD; fiscal year ended December 31, 2025 (FY2025), unless stated otherwise. Nu Holdings Ltd. is Cayman-incorporated and a NYSE foreign private issuer. Cayman law does not require individual management-pay disclosure, so the filing reports directors and key-management compensation only in aggregate. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6B — Compensation of Directors and Management Team, p.194]

## 1. Compensation Structure

| Executive | Base | Annual Bonus | Long-Term Incentive | Cash vs Equity Mix | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| CEO — David Vélez Osorno | Not individually disclosed | Not individually disclosed; Brazil-based management may participate in a union-negotiated Profit Sharing Program | Annual RSU awards; individual award size and vesting terms not disclosed | Not quantified. The program says it intends a majority of management compensation to be share-based, but gives no CEO-specific split. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6B — Executive Compensation, pp.194–195] |
| CFO — Guilherme Lago in FY2025 | Not individually disclosed | Not individually disclosed | Not individually disclosed | Not quantified; same management-program disclosure only. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6B — Executive Compensation, pp.194–195] |

The only disclosed pay total is **US$91.3m** for all directors and other key management in FY2025, including controlled-company payments, versus **US$96.0m** in FY2024: a US$4.7m, **4.9%** decline. It is not CEO or CFO pay and cannot be used as either executive's compensation total. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6B — Directors and Key Management Compensation, p.194; FY2024 Form 20-F, Item 6B — Directors and Key Management Compensation]

## 2. Performance Metrics

| Plan | Metric | Weight | Per-Share/Returns or Size-Based? | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|
| Annual bonus / Profit Sharing Program | Pre-established business goals negotiated through Brazilian employee-union agreements; the filing says Brazil officers use the same formulas and maximum payouts as other Brazilian employees. Goal names and outcomes are not disclosed. | Not disclosed | **Unclassifiable from the filing.** It does not establish a revenue, EBITDA, ROIC, EPS, FCF/share, or TSR measure. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6B — Profit Sharing, p.195] |
| Long-term incentive | Annual RSUs. The company says executive grants consider individual performance, business-unit performance, overall company performance and the importance of performance to long-term goals; it does not name indicators or weights. Most outstanding RSUs generally vest on service/time conditions. | Not disclosed | **Mixed but not an explicit returns/per-share scorecard.** Time-vested equity exposes holders to the share price and supports retention, but the filing does not prove ROIC, EPS, FCF/share, TSR, revenue, EBITDA, deal-count, or headcount weighting. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6B — Share-Based Compensation, p.195; Description of Share Capital — RSUs, p.212] |

No absolute-size metric is disclosed. That is not proof that one does not exist; it means the filing cannot show whether the annual goals reward per-share value, total company size, or both. The metric-and-weight gap is the limiting fact in this assessment.

## 3. Pay vs Performance

| Signal | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| CEO total pay (latest) | Not disclosed individually. The US$91.3m FY2025 figure is the aggregate for directors and other key management, not CEO pay. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6B — Directors and Key Management Compensation, p.194] |
| Pay vs peers (if available) | Not assessable. Independent consultants use a peer group based on fintech/software/internet or finance, similar market capitalization and revenue, growth, and talent competition, but the filing does not name the peer group or show percentile/pay outcomes. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6B — Peer Group, pp.194–195] |
| Pay-for-performance alignment | Mixed. There is a variable profit-sharing program and long-dated equity, but individual payouts, metrics, weights, targets, and target-versus-actual results are not disclosed. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6B — Compensation Composition and Share-Based Compensation, pp.195–196] |
| Say-on-pay support (%) | Not assessable. No vote-support figure or response to remuneration dissent is in the supplied FY2025 Form 20-F or AGM materials. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6B, pp.194–199 — reviewed disclosure] |

## 3A. Statutory & Code Pay Gates (A6-01, A6-02, A6-05, A6-06, A6-09)

| Test | Raw value | Band applied | Verdict | Source |
|---|---:|---|---|---|
| Aggregate managerial pay vs the statutory cap (India: Sec 197 — 11% aggregate; ≤5% single MD/WTD, ≤10% all executives, NEDs ≤1%/3%; loss years per Schedule V) | US$91.3m aggregate directors/key management; individual sums unavailable | Not Applicable — India Sec. 197 thresholds do not apply to this Cayman issuer; no alternative statutory cap is evidenced in the pool | Not Applicable | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6B — Directors and Key Management Compensation, p.194] |

Board discretion to accelerate awards prevents a clean double-trigger conclusion; no gross-up or guaranteed-bonus disclosure found. | Amber | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Omnibus Incentive Plan — Amendment and Change in Control, pp.198–199] |

## 3B. Human Capital (A12-01 … A12-04)

| Test | Raw value | Band applied | Verdict | Source |
|---|---:|---|---|---|
| Attrition vs industry median | Employee count: 10,027 in 2025 versus 8,716 in 2024 (+15.0%); attrition rate not disclosed | Headcount growth is not attrition, so no industry comparison is possible | Insufficient Data | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6D — Employees, p.200] |
| Median-employee pay level & change vs executive change | Median employee pay not disclosed; CEO/CFO pay not disclosed | Cannot test pay divergence | Insufficient Data | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6B and Item 6D, pp.194, 200] |
| ESOP pool breadth (broad-based vs top-concentrated); promoters excluded? | Approximately 88% of active employees had received share-based awards at December 31, 2025; 37.616m RSUs granted in 2025 | Broad workforce coverage. But the Omnibus Plan is also open to officers and non-employee directors, and individual founder/CEO grants are not disclosed. | Amber | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Business — World-Class Talent; Note 10 — Share-based payments, F-45–F-46; Item 6B — Omnibus Incentive Plan, pp.196–197] |
| Workplace-conduct record (POSH / labor / safety, disclosed + resolved) | US$3.795m labor-risk provision at December 31, 2025; Code of Conduct and whistleblower policy exist, but no workplace-complaint, harassment, discrimination, safety, or resolution statistics are disclosed | A labor provision is not evidence of misconduct. The policies do not provide the outcome data needed to clear the test. | Insufficient Data | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Note 25 — Provisions and contingent liabilities, F-75; Item 16B — Code of Ethics, p.240] |

The equity dilution test is favorable on a matched FY2025 filing basis: RSUs granted were **37.616m / 4,855.674m ordinary shares = 0.775%** annual burn; outstanding options plus RSUs were **21.819m + 53.088m = 74.908m / 4,855.674m = 1.543%** overhang. Both clear the module's <2% burn and <10% overhang Green bands. The plan also says it is intended not to exceed 5% of fully diluted shares at a time. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Note 10 — Share-based payments, F-45–F-46; Item 6B — Omnibus Incentive Plan, p.196; Description of Share Capital — Options and RSUs, p.212]

The deterministic CIQ read reports 4,830.7m shares outstanding as of August 29, 2026, 25.0m (0.51%) below the FY2025 filing's 4,855.674m year-end denominator. This is a date-basis difference, not a material extraction conflict; the annual burn and overhang calculations above deliberately use the filing's FY2025 denominator. [CIQ Comps → Financial Data, Shares Outstanding Latest, as of 2026-08-29 — vendor export; FY2025 Form 20-F, Description of Share Capital — issued and outstanding ordinary shares, p.210]

One small within-filing reconciliation also does not change the conclusion: Item 7 says 53.104m RSUs outstanding while Note 10's audited roll-forward says 53.088m, a 15,874-share (0.03% of RSUs) difference. The calculation uses Note 10 because it provides the full grant roll-forward.

## 4. Incentive Alignment Read

**Mixed.** Nu pays through salary, a short-term profit-sharing program and mostly equity-based long-term awards, so management has share-price exposure and the employee plan is broad. But the disclosed RSU program is mainly time-based and the 20-F withholds individual pay, performance targets, weights, and outcomes; it therefore does not prove that executives are paid for per-share value rather than growth or other company goals.

The single biggest signal is that Nu's long-term incentive is retention-oriented, with a 3.8-year weighted-average vesting period, rather than a published ROIC, EPS, FCF/share, or TSR scorecard. That makes the structure better than cash-only pay but leaves the actual performance incentive mixed. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Note 10 — Share-based payments, F-45–F-47; Item 6B — Share-Based Compensation, p.195]

## management-governance / 04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md

_Source: `04_ownership-and-insider-behavior.md`_

# Ownership & Insider Behavior — NU

**Scope and basis.** NU is a Cayman Islands company whose investable primary line is Class A ordinary shares on the NYSE. Its annual ownership disclosure is the FY2025 Form 20-F, not an Indian promoter filing. Amounts below are shares unless stated otherwise. The filing's ownership table is as of December 31, 2025, with the stated share-count denominator as of March 1, 2026; Capital IQ holdings are later vendor snapshots and are not substituted for the filing.

## 1. Ownership

| Holder | % Held | Share Class | Bought or Granted? | Source |
|---|---:|---|---|---|
| CEO / founder — David Vélez Osorno | 18.76% economic interest in the latest vendor snapshot; 74.4% of voting power in the filed table | 3.088m Class A plus 903.124m Class B in the filed table | Founder status is disclosed, but the available filing does not disclose cash paid or a purchase history. Do not treat the stake as open-market buying. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, p.203; CIQ Public Ownership Detailed, latest holder record 2026-07-23 — vendor export] |
| Other insiders (inclusive total) | 22.80% of shares outstanding classified as Individuals/Insiders; includes David and Cristina, so it is not additive | Mixed Class A / Class B / awards | Purchase-versus-grant basis is not available for the aggregate. | [CIQ Public Ownership Summary, 2026-08-29 — vendor export] |
| Cristina Junqueira — co-founder, CGO and U.S. CEO | 2.69% economic interest in latest vendor snapshot; 9.9% voting power in the filed table | 11.427m Class A plus 119.476m Class B in filed table | Historical cash-acquisition basis is not disclosed. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, p.203; CIQ Public Ownership Detailed, latest holder record 2026-08-25 — vendor export] |
| Largest institutional holders | BlackRock 7.35%; Baillie Gifford 4.76%; Capital Research 3.21% | Class A equivalents | Secondary-market positions; not insider holdings. | [CIQ Public Ownership Detailed, position dates 2026-06-30 — vendor export] |
| Promoter / controlling bloc | David Vélez and affiliates: 88.3% of Class B, 74.4% of all voting power; his 906.213m filed shares equal 18.6% of the 4,860.599m stated Class A+B denominator | Class B has 20 votes per share; Class A has one | Historic acquisition price is not disclosed. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, pp.202–204] |

Total insider ownership is **22.80%** in Capital IQ's latest classification as of 2026-08-29. The primary filing establishes the more important distinction: David's **18.6% economic interest** carried **74.4% voting power**. The arithmetic is 74.4% less 18.6%, or a **55.8 percentage-point control-versus-cash-flow wedge**; the difference arises from Class B's 20 votes per share, not from a pyramid. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, pp.202–204]

The available evidence establishes founder exposure but not how much of the founders' holdings was bought with cash rather than received before listing. The 2026 transaction record instead shows equity awards and a low-price derivative exercise, which are compensation or exercise events rather than open-market conviction buys. [CIQ Public Ownership → Insider Trading, through 2026-08-26 — vendor export]

## 2. Insider Transactions (last 12 months)

| Insider | Buy / Sell | Amount | Date | Notes (10b5-1?) | Source |
|---|---|---:|---|---|---|
| Cristina Junqueira | Open-market sell | 350,000 Class A; US$5.23m | 2026-03-23 and 2026-08-14 | Two sales at US$14.81–14.82 and US$15.80. The vendor export does not state whether either was under a Rule 10b5-1 plan. | [CIQ Public Ownership → Insider Trading, Form 4 rows filed 2026-03-26 and 2026-08-18 — vendor export] |
| Henrique Fragelli, CRO | Open-market sell | 221,707 Class A; US$3.51m | 2026-08-14 | US$15.81; plan status not stated in the vendor export. | [CIQ Public Ownership → Insider Trading, Form 4 row filed 2026-08-18 — vendor export] |
| Anita Sands, lead independent director | Open-market sell | 21,000 Class A; US$0.26m | 2026-05-15 | US$12.24; plan status not stated in the vendor export. | [CIQ Public Ownership → Insider Trading, Form 4 row filed 2026-05-20 — vendor export] |
| David Vélez, Cristina Junqueira, Henrique Fragelli and Guilherme Marques do Lago | Sale to issuer, not open market | 157,527 Class A; US$2.20m | 2026-04-23 and 2026-07-23 | The export labels these “Sale to Issuer”; it does not give the purpose, so tax withholding is not assumed. | [CIQ Public Ownership → Insider Trading, Form 4 rows filed 2026-04-27 and 2026-07-27 — vendor export] |
| Rob Livingston, CFO | Restricted-share-unit acquisition | 2,194,587 RSUs; US$0 reported value | 2026-07-13 | Award, not an open-market purchase. | [CIQ Public Ownership → Insider Trading, Form 4 row filed 2026-07-15 — vendor export] |
| Henrique Fragelli, CRO | Derivative exercise and retained stock | 2,361,450 Class A; US$0.29–0.43 exercise range | 2026-04-08 | Exercise, not an open-market purchase. | [CIQ Public Ownership → Insider Trading, Form 4 row filed 2026-04-10 — vendor export] |
| Eight non-employee directors | RSU awards | 204,124 RSUs; US$0 reported value | 2026-08-07 | Awards, not open-market purchases. | [CIQ Public Ownership → Insider Trading, Form 4 rows filed 2026-08-12 — vendor export] |

**Net insider activity: net selling on the relevant open-market measure.** The deterministic CIQ facts sidecar reports **0 open-market buys and 4 open-market sales, net -592,707 shares**, through 2026-08-18. Its all-transaction total is instead **net +4,015,012 shares** through 2026-08-26, but that total includes RSUs, other acquisitions and the derivative exercise above; it is not evidence of US$ cash buying. [CIQ Public Ownership → Insider Trading “Open Market Acq/Disp,” trailing 12m to 2026-08-18 — vendor export; CIQ Public Ownership → Insider Trading “Transacted Shares,” trailing 12m to 2026-08-26 — vendor export]

The 300,000-share March sale by Junqueira came 52 days before Q1 2026 results. Capital IQ's quarter record reports a -5.3% GAAP-EPS surprise for that quarter. This is a timing lead, not a market-conduct finding: the pool contains a vendor transcription of the Form 4, not the underlying filing or a disclosed 10b5-1 plan, so RF-MKT-001 is not fired. [CIQ Public Ownership → Insider Trading, Form 4 row filed 2026-03-26 — vendor export; Beat / Miss Setup — NU, §4, 2026-08-30]

## 3. Pledging & Encumbrance

| Insider | Shares Pledged | % of Their Holding | Source |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| David Vélez / Rua California Ltd. | Not disclosed | Not assessable | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, pp.202–204] |
| Cristina Junqueira and disclosed holder entities | Not disclosed | Not assessable | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, pp.202–204] |

**No pledging disclosed.** Item 7 identifies voting and investment power and the entities holding the founders' shares, but does not provide a pledged-share numerator or an affirmative no-pledge statement. This is therefore not a reported 0% pledge ratio. The business-model disqualifier scan reached the same conclusion and found no >50% insider-pledge trigger. No RF-OWN-001 or RF-OWN-002 is fired. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, pp.202–204; Business-model Disqualifier Scan — NU, §1, 2026-08-30]

## 4. Control Structure

| Feature | Detail | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Dual-class / super-voting | Class B carries 20 votes per share versus one for Class A. David held 88.3% of Class B and 74.4% of combined voting power as of the filed record date. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, pp.202–204] |
| Controlled-company status | NU says it is a NYSE “controlled company” because David held 74.3% of voting power as of the annual-report date. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, Controlled Company Exception, p.201] |
| Shareholder bloc with board rights | The 2025-amended Shareholder's Agreement gives David up to five nominees/a board majority above 40% voting power, three nominees above 25%, and one above 5%; it also gives committee-designation rights. At 74.4%, he is above the majority-nomination threshold. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, Shareholder's Agreement, p.193] |
| Consent / veto rights | Above 10% voting power, David's prior written approval is required for specified matters including major debt, related-party actions, strategy, dividends, equity issuance, M&A, change of control, assets above 20% of net equity, and officer/director compensation. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, Shareholder's Agreement, p.193] |
| Controlling-owner objective alignment *(Filter 6)* | NU is not evidenced to be government-controlled, a listed subsidiary of a value-maximizing parent, or a sprawling unrelated conglomerate; RF-OWN-004 does not apply. David's commercial founder stake supplies real exposure, but the dual-class and contractual rights leave Class A holders unable to block his decisions. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp.187, 193 and 201; Item 7A, p.203] |

The direct control risk is not an allegation of misconduct. It is a durable minority-rights constraint: a holder of Class A has one vote per share while David can elect a majority of the board and approve specified actions alone under the agreement. It should lower the control-structure-fairness component; it is not the separate government/parent/conglomerate conflict required for RF-OWN-004. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, p.193; Item 7A, pp.202–204]

No reclassification review is available. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, pp.202–204] |

The chain is named, although an independent registry sweep of every holder entity is outside this agent's evidence. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, p.203, footnotes 2–4] |
| Takeover-code disclosure hygiene (India: SAST Reg 29 timeliness, creeping-acquisition limits, promoter ≤75%) | India SAST thresholds do not apply. The filing cites BlackRock's November 2024 and Baillie Gifford's April 2025 Schedule 13G disclosures, but the pool does not contain a full SEC-filing-timeliness review for all material holders or controller changes. | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, p.203, footnotes 5–6] |

## 5. Ownership / Insider Read

NU has meaningful founder economic ownership — 22.80% for all insiders in the latest vendor classification — but the record does not establish how much founder stock was cash-bought rather than granted before listing. The behavioral signal is net open-market selling: four sales and no buys, while the apparent all-type net acquisition is mostly awards and an exercise. The largest minority-holder risk is David Vélez's 74.4% voting control from an 18.6% economic stake, reinforced by board-nomination and consent rights; pledging remains unassessable rather than clean.

## management-governance / 05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md

_Source: `05_board-and-shareholder-rights.md`_

# Board & Shareholder Rights — NU

**Scope and standard.** Nu Holdings Ltd. is a Cayman Islands company and a foreign private issuer (FPI) listed on the NYSE. This is therefore a Cayman/NYSE-FPI rights review, not an Indian LODR review. “Independent” below means the company’s disclosed designation; it is not a conclusion that the director is independent of the controlling shareholder. The latest primary board disclosure is nine directors as of 8 April 2026 and the 6 August 2026 AGM re-elected those nine. A Capital IQ roster dated 29 August adds Roberto Campos Neto as a director, which conflicts with both primary disclosures and the vendor’s own disclosed maximum board size of nine. I do not treat that vendor row as proof of a tenth current director. The current post-AGM board count therefore needs a company confirmation.

**Verdict: Weak / entrenched.** The Audit and Risk Committee is fully independent and a lead independent director exists, but David Vélez’s 74.3% voting power, 20:1 Class B votes, right to nominate up to five directors, and consent rights over major decisions mean the Class A vote cannot independently constrain the controller. The overall governance finding is not an integrity allegation.

## 1. Board Composition

| Director / official position | Company-designated independent | Committee role | First disclosed board service | Outside listed-board load disclosed | Rights / independence read | Evidence |
|---|---:|---|---|---:|---|---|
| David Vélez Osorno — Founder, Chairman and CEO | No | None | Founder since 2013; exact director start not separately disclosed | 0 disclosed | Combined chair/CEO and controller; not independent | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp.187–188] |
| Anita Mary Sands — Lead Independent Director | Yes | Audit & Risk | Oct. 2020; lead independent since Oct. 2022 | 1 (ServiceNow) | Lead-independent counterweight, but elected under controller voting power | [FY2025 Form 20-F, pp.187–188] |
| David Alexandre Marcus — Director | No designation | Compensation & People | Mar. 2023 | 0 disclosed | Not designated independent; committee membership is a protection gap | [FY2025 Form 20-F, pp.187–189] |
| Douglas Mauro Leone — Director | Yes | Compensation & People | 2016 | 0 disclosed | Long tenure and extensive private-company commitments merit monitoring | [FY2025 Form 20-F, pp.187–189] |
| Diego Piacentini — Director | Yes | None | Apr. 2026 | 1 (DoorDash) | Recent addition provides some refreshment | [FY2025 Form 20-F, pp.187–189] |
| Jacqueline Dawn Reses — Director | Yes | Chair, Compensation & People | Mar. 2021 | 1 (Affirm; TaskUs status not independently confirmed) | Committee chair is independent by company designation | [FY2025 Form 20-F, pp.187–189] |
| Luis Alberto Moreno Mejía — Director | Yes | Compensation & People | Apr. 2021 | 1 (Dow) | Company-designated independent | [FY2025 Form 20-F, pp.187–190] |
| Rogério Paulo Calderón Peres — Director | Yes | Chair, Audit & Risk | Jun. 2021 | 2 (Alupar; Grupo Casas Bahia) | Audit chair; disclosed financial expert | [FY2025 Form 20-F, pp.187–190] |
| Thuan Quang Pham — Director | Yes | Audit & Risk | Sep. 2022 | 0 disclosed | Company-designated independent | [FY2025 Form 20-F, pp.187–190] |

The primary record gives seven company-designated independent directors out of nine (77.8%), a non-independent founder chair/CEO, and one other non-independent director. That is a useful formal majority, but it does not neutralise the controller’s voting, nomination, and consent rights. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp.187–194; Item 7A, p.203]

### 1A. Per-director assessment

| David Marcus | Not designated independent; no further reason disclosed | c.3.5 years; no disclosed outside listed board | Clean, Tier-C coverage limited | Amber |
| Douglas Leone | Designated independent; no disclosed controller/family tie | c.10 years; substantial private-board list disclosed | Clean, Tier-C coverage limited | Amber |

“Clean” above is not full clearance: the people-integrity review found no attributed Material or Disqualifying fact for the controller, CEO, CFO, chair, or directors, but coverage was limited rather than a complete-person register. [Management-Governance 07 People & Integrity Dossiers, 2026-08-30, A1-05]

### 1B. Board Process & Renewal

| Finding ID | Test | Read | Evidence and implication |
|---|---|---|---|
| A1-08 | Skills matrix | Amber | Biographies show banking, audit, technology, payments, risk, and people experience, but Nu does not disclose a director-by-director skills matrix or a formal gap assessment. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp.188–190] |
| A1-09 | Resignations / exits | Insufficient Data | No director-resignation history, reasons, or 24-month exit analysis is disclosed in the reviewed materials. Absence of disclosure is not evidence of no resignations. |
| A1-10 | Reappointment quality | Amber | All nine named directors were re-elected at the 6 August 2026 AGM, but the available result does not provide director-by-director for/against/abstention figures or a response to dissent. Controller voting prevents this from being an independent shareholder endorsement. [Nu Holdings Form 6-K, Exhibit 99.1, 2026-08-06] |
| A1-11 | Board evaluation | Amber | Capital IQ records that a board performance review exists, but no method, findings, actions, or recurrence evidence is disclosed. [Capital IQ Corporate Governance export, as of 2026-08-29 — vendor] |
| A1-12 | Diversity and refreshment | Amber | Two of nine officially disclosed directors are women (Sands and Reses); Piacentini joined in Apr. 2026. Median disclosed tenure is about 5.3 years, but no formal diversity/refreshment policy or outcome is disclosed. Leone’s c.10-year tenure merits monitoring, not a finding of non-independence by itself. [FY2025 Form 20-F, pp.187–190] |
| A1-13 | Succession | Amber | The Compensation & People charter covers management succession and the vendor field says a CEO succession plan exists, but no plan design, board process, or founder-CEO contingency disclosure is available. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6C, pp.200–201; Capital IQ Corporate Governance export, as of 2026-08-29 — vendor] |
| A1-14 | Board meetings / quorum | Insufficient Data | Meeting count, spacing, director attendance, and quorum compliance are not disclosed in the reviewed primary materials. |
| A1-15 | Election / renewal mechanics | Amber | Directors stand for election annually until the controller falls below 50% voting power. That is better than a permanent board term, but the controller can determine the result and has contractual nomination rights. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, p.187; Item 6A “Shareholders Agreement”, pp.193–194] |
| A1-16 | Minority approval of independent directors | Not Applicable / protection absent | The Indian public-shareholder vote rule does not govern this Cayman FPI. No equivalent Class-A or disinterested-minority approval for independent-director appointments is disclosed. |
| A1-17 | Independent sessions / D&O cover | Amber | Nu relies on the FPI exemption from certain NYSE governance requirements, but its vendor-recorded policy calls for non-management and independent sessions at least twice a year; actual sessions are not disclosed. Nu reports D&O insurance, but calls the policy limit “minimal.” [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 16G; Item 6B, p.194; Capital IQ Corporate Governance export, as of 2026-08-29 — vendor] |

## 2. Tenure & Refreshment

The official board has one founder, seven company-designated independent directors, and one director not designated independent. The newest director joined in April 2026. The board’s disclosed median tenure is about 5.3 years, while Leone has served since 2016. Neither the long-serving director nor the controller link is, by itself, proof of compromised judgment. The concern is structural: the founder can nominate up to five directors while holding at least 40% of the vote, and he currently holds 74.3%. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp.187–194; Item 7A, p.203]

Director attendance, individual commitment time, age/retirement policy, evaluation results, and reasons for any recent board exits are not disclosed. These are real evidence gaps. Capital IQ’s current roster conflict also means the board count after the AGM should be reconciled to an official Nu disclosure before treating the roster as fully current. [Capital IQ Board Members export, as of 2026-08-29 — vendor]

## 3. RPT & Group Leakage

This is a rights and disclosure read only; quantitative related-party materiality and the full A5 score belong to Management-Governance 09.

Nu says credit cards, loans, deposits, and investments involving executive directors, board members, key employees, and close family are on similar terms to unrelated third parties and have normal collectability risk. It also says intragroup balances and transactions are eliminated on consolidation. Those statements are useful, but they do not identify a transaction-by-transaction approval record. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Note 28, p.F-77]

The disclosed non-routine related-party item is a commercial relationship entered in Q2 2024 with a company whose CEO is one of Nu’s directors. Nu received a cash incentive to support project costs subject to conditions. The related entity and director are not named, and the filing does not state the approving committee, recusal, competitive process, or the conditions. The associated other-related-party liability was US$0.926m at 31 December 2025, down from US$1.795m at 31 December 2024; the interim balance was US$0.265m at 30 June 2026. This is not evidence of misconduct, but it leaves a minority-holder verification gap. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Note 28, p.F-77; Q2 2026 Interim Report, Note 28, p.34]

| RPT right / basis | FY2025 | 30 Jun. 2026 | Matched-basis conclusion |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| Other liability from the disclosed director-linked commercial relationship | US$(0.926)m, period-end | US$(0.265)m, period-end | The two balance-sheet dates are comparable; liability reduced by US$0.661m. This is not a revenue, profit, or transaction-volume ratio. |
| Sales, purchases, loans, guarantees, service fees, and transaction flow with the director-linked counterparty | Not separately quantified | Not separately quantified | Not assessable from the disclosure; do not infer “none.” |
| Specific Audit & Risk / board approval and director recusal for that item | Not disclosed | Not disclosed | Not assessable. The general committee remit is not evidence of approval of this transaction. |
| Intragroup relationship rows in the supply-chain graph | 0 of 11 disclosed relationship rows | n/a | The graph is scope-limited to recently disclosed supplier/customer links and has group entities; it is not an RPT register or an arm’s-length certification. [relationships.json, 2026-08-30 — Capital IQ vendor graph] |

Audit & Risk’s charter includes reviewing related-party transactions, and all three listed members are company-designated independent. That is a meaningful process safeguard, but it does not cure the missing transaction-specific approval and disclosure. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6C, pp.199–200]

## 4. Takeover Defenses & Voting Rights

Nu has a dual-class structure: each Class A share has one vote and each Class B share has 20 votes. At 1 March 2026, Vélez held 88.3% of Class B shares and 74.3% of the combined voting power. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, p.203]

The shareholder agreement gives the founder, while he holds at least 40% of voting power, the right to designate up to five directors. At at least 10%, it also requires his prior written consent for material debt, related-party transactions involving officers/directors other than him, major business changes, a rights plan, dividends, a change of control, liquidation, share-capital changes, asset sales above 20% of net equity, and director/officer compensation. With 74.3% voting power, he exceeds both thresholds. These rights are more restrictive than simple voting control: they give a contractual consent layer over board and shareholder decisions. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A “Shareholders Agreement”, pp.193–194]

Class B holders, but not Class A holders, have preemptive rights on future ordinary-share issuance to maintain proportional ownership. Nu also has authorized blank-check preferred shares, with none outstanding, that the board may issue without a shareholder vote. These features can dilute Class A holders or entrench control, although no preferred issuance or active poison pill was disclosed. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 10B “Ordinary Shares—Preemptive or Similar Rights”; Capital IQ Takeover Defenses export, as of 2026-08-29 — vendor]

There is one mitigating legal feature: if a merger, consolidation, or sale of all/substantially all assets occurs, the Class A and Class B shares must receive equal treatment. That does not remove the founder’s power to decide whether such a transaction proceeds. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 10B “Equal Status”]

| Right / defense | Minority-holder read | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| 20:1 dual class | Very weak: Class A economic owners do not have comparable voting influence | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, p.203] |
| Controller board nomination | Weak: up to five founder designees at ≥40% voting; current voting exceeds threshold | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp.193–194] |
| Controller prior-consent rights | Weak: broad veto over capital, control, strategy, pay, debt, and specified RPTs at ≥10% voting | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp.193–194] |
| Class B preemptive rights only | Weak: future issuances can preserve the controller’s stake while Class A lacks the same right | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 10B] |
| Classified board | Conditional defense: it starts only if founder voting falls below 50%, with founder nominees allocated longest initial classes | [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, p.187] |
| Blank-check preferred shares | Dilution / defense capacity exists; no issuance currently disclosed | [Capital IQ Takeover Defenses export, as of 2026-08-29 — vendor] |
| Poison pill | No active pill reported; the shareholder agreement requires controller consent for one | [Capital IQ Takeover Defenses export, as of 2026-08-29 — vendor; FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp.193–194] |

### 4A. Shareholder Voting & Dilution

Directors are elected annually while the founder remains above the classifying threshold; the founder’s vote can determine the outcome. Shareholders require 50% of voting power to requisition a special meeting. If the founder ceases to hold a majority, the articles provide that shareholders may not requisition a meeting. Written resolutions require unanimity of shareholders, and there is no cumulative voting. These provisions make a Class-A-led challenge impractical in the present structure. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 10B “General Meetings of Shareholders” and “Voting Rights”; Capital IQ Corporate Governance export, as of 2026-08-29 — vendor]

Amendments to the memorandum and articles require a two-thirds special resolution. That ordinarily constrains unilateral board action, but the founder’s 74.3% voting interest can meet that threshold. The board cannot itself amend the charter according to the vendor export; this is a formal protection whose practical value is limited by control concentration. [Capital IQ Corporate Governance export, as of 2026-08-29 — vendor; FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 7A, p.203]

### 4B. Corporate-Actions Fairness

| Finding ID | Event / test | Conclusion |
|---|---|---|
| A10-02 | M&A / share exchange fairness | No material M&A, merger, or share-exchange proposal in the reviewed period. Not assessable. The equal-treatment provision is a legal safeguard, but controller approval power remains. |
| A10-03 | AGM vote fairness and opposition | Amber. The 6 August 2026 AGM re-elected nine directors and approved the annual report/financial statements, but the available official result does not provide itemized opposition or a board response to dissent. 94.30% of outstanding shares were represented according to the shareholder-call transcript. [Nu Holdings Form 6-K, Exhibit 99.1, 2026-08-06; S&P Capital IQ Shareholder/Analyst Call transcript, 2026-08-06 — vendor transcript] |
| A10-04 | Dilution / capital raise fairness | Amber. No reviewed selective raise or warrant issue establishes an unfair price. However, Class-B-only preemptive rights and blank-check preferred authority are standing dilution asymmetries. |
| A10-06 | Delisting fairness | Not Applicable. No delisting, going-private, or comparable control transaction is disclosed in the reviewed period. |

## 5. Minority Protection Read

**Conclusion: weak / entrenched.** Nu’s formal board design has positive features: seven of nine directors are company-designated independent, the lead independent director sits on the Audit & Risk Committee, that committee is fully independent, and its chair is the disclosed financial expert. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp.187–190; Item 6C, pp.199–200]

Those safeguards are outweighed for Class A holders by the controller’s current 74.3% vote, 20:1 Class B votes, ability to nominate a board majority, and broad contractual consent rights. The absence of a separate nomination committee, the presence of a non-independent member on the Compensation & People Committee, Class-B-only preemptive rights, and missing attendance/evaluation/dissent detail further weaken outside holders’ ability to test board accountability. [FY2025 Form 20-F, Item 6A, pp.193–194; Item 7A, p.203; Item 6C, pp.199–201]

The critical watch item is not a proven abuse. It is whether Nu can demonstrate actual independent challenge: current official board composition; meeting and attendance records; independent-session occurrence; named approval and recusal for the director-linked commercial relationship; and AGM vote results by nominee and proposal.

## Board Score

| Component | Score | Why |
|---|---:|---|
| Board composition and formal independence | 12 / 20 | Seven designated independent directors and a lead independent director; founder is chair/CEO/controller |
| True independence, tenure, workload, renewal | 7 / 15 | No disclosed controller/family ties for designated independents, but tenure/workload evidence and formal independence testing are incomplete |
| Committee design | 11 / 15 | Fully independent Audit & Risk Committee and financial expert; no nomination committee; Compensation & People not fully independent |
| Board process transparency | 3 / 10 | No attendance, cadence, quorum, evaluation outcome, or dissent disclosure |
| RPT rights and disclosure | 8 / 10 | General Audit & Risk oversight and normal-terms statement; director-linked item lacks named approval/recusal/terms |
| Voting, dilution, and takeover rights | 1 / 20 | 20:1 dual class, 74.3% controller vote, nomination/consent rights, Class-B-only preemption, preferred authority |
| AGM and corporate-action fairness | 4 / 10 | Annual election and equal treatment in a sale; controller can determine votes and detailed opposition is unavailable |
| **Board & shareholder-rights score** | **46 / 100** | **Mixed formal board safeguards, but weak / entrenched minority-holder protection** |

A 46/100 score reflects a governance structure that is not a documented fraud or integrity failure. It does mean that a Class A holder has limited practical power to replace directors, block dilution, call a meeting, or force a strategic decision against the controller. This is a material governance cap for the module synthesis.
